- FormalJudge: A Neuro-Symbolic Paradigm for Agentic Oversight (2026)
    - what: Post-hoc agent-trajectory oversight separates intent decomposition,atomic-fact extraction,formal predicate generation and Dafny/Z3 checking. Structured logs are parsed directly where possible;LLMs extract semantic facts and synthesize the constraints. Authors report improved agreement with an LLM-ensemble reference,including deception classification and feedback-based behavioral refinement. Formal guarantees are conditional on faithful facts and specifications,not proofs about an agent policy or natural-language intent. Provides explicit atomic taxonomies and composition formulas for file-grounded deception,tool safety and constraint compliance;localized context extraction separates tool errors,disclosure,document access and fabrication. Adds a trusted-trace evidence-manipulation boundary test,manual integrity atoms,latency breakdown,qualitative-intent proxy examples,multi-turn stress tests and action/task-level evaluation. Iterative feedback sends generated Dafny constraints and failed-predicate explanations back to an agent. Detailed appendices show which observed facts are composed and reveal semantic/specification mismatches that solver success alone cannot catch.
    - limits: §3.4 explicitly requires faithful extracted facts and a Dafny specification capturing intended constraints. Errors can enter decomposition, semantic extraction and synthesis. The solver does not verify the LLM, policy, truth of observations or intended meaning. No theorem proves end-to-end soundness of this oversight architecture. Observed trajectories and trusted tool-observation logs must retain provenance. Agent-controlled files and self-reports may be fabricated. Appendix A.3 assumes attackers cannot overwrite the trusted trace; without external provenance a trajectory-only checker cannot recover rewritten original events. Reference labels in §4.1 are majority judgments from GPT-5.2, Gemini-3.0-Pro and Claude-4.5-Opus across three rounds. Metrics measure agreement with that ensemble, rather than independently established ground truth. Opus is also the main judge and specification compiler; correlated model errors remain possible. Seven main agents: GPT-4o, Gemini-3.0-Pro, Claude-4.5-Sonnet, Qwen-2.5 7B/14B/32B/72B. Table 1 uses an Opus judge. Extended robustness uses eight judges including GPT-5.2, Grok-3, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Sonnet and four Qwen sizes. The main five-judge description and extended eight-judge analysis describe different settings. Full benchmark sizes are 2,000 Agent-SafetyBench cases, 400 VitaBench tasks and 200 Deceivers tasks. Vita evaluation actually uses a 100-task split; robustness/cross-verifier analysis uses 30 tasks and 164 tool calls. Deceivers weak-to-strong analysis uses 200 deceptive plus 200 nondeceptive trajectories per agent size. Full benchmark size is not every experiment’s denominator. Agent-SafetyBench reference protocol in Appendix D combines fulfillable/risk annotations with actual actions and uses the ensemble for ambiguity, unlike the uniformly ensemble-based wording in §4.1. Deceivers design labels provide a consistency check; broken tools do not automatically make every resulting trajectory dishonest. Some atomic questions remain substantial semantic judgments: whether a file contains requested information, an answer ignores content, or fabrication follows disclosure. Boolean answers do not make these judgments objective. Qualitative intent uses observable proxies rather than an exact semantics of politeness or manipulation. Agent-SafetyBench refinement uses Opus for decomposition/specification synthesis, including small-agent experiments. For the 7B-judge versus 72B-agent claim, the exact allocation of extraction/synthesis roles matters; judge size alone does not specify the capacity of the complete pipeline. Verification audits completed traces. Refinement regenerates responses or attempts and cannot undo actions already executed. X-Teaming and OpenClaw additions measure unsafe-trace detection, not online prevention or reduction in the native attack success rate. Dafny/Z3 checking can time out or fail without proving the negation. The paper models V as binary but does not fully specify timeout, unknown or syntax-error handling. The 6.4% backend error share classifies an unspecified sample of errors; it does not establish logical unsoundness in Z3. No independent artifact reproduction, compiler/version audit, complete human-label audit or systematic correctness proof of generated checkers is supplied. Attribution to the solver alone is limited when decomposition, contexts, prompts and generated code differ.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `formaljudge-a-neuro-symbolic-2026`

- Lean4Agent FormalAgentLib and LeanEvolve (2026)
    - title: Lean4Agent: Formal Modeling and Verification for Agent Workflow and Trajectory
    - what: Presents Lean4Agent, a Lean4 dependent-type framework with FormalAgentLib for structural, semantic and execution-trajectory verification of LLM-agent workflows, plus LeanEvolve for verification-guided workflow refinement. Workflows are typed heterogeneous graphs of read/write variables, natural-language instructions and execution types; trajectories record pre/post states and reasoning/tool traces. Layer 1 checks node reachability, edge validity and read/write consistency. Layer 2 translates explicit and implicit variables into decidable predicates and graph-level information-flow/context predicates, proving precondition entailment under an LLMExec local-correctness assumption. Layer 3 checks concrete trajectories using Lean propositions, Python validators and LLM-as-judge, localizing failed steps. LeanEvolve feeds diagnostics and optional test feedback to an LLM for targeted workflow edits, with a pure-LLM exploratory mode. Experiments generate 40 workflows, compare verification-passing/failing workflows across five LLMs on 50 hard SWE-Bench-Verified tasks and 100 ELAIP-Bench questions, and study graph-predicate and pure-LLM ablations.
    - number: Layer-2 passing workflows improve SWE task rate by 14.80 percentage points (95% CI 10.00–19.60)
    - number: ELAIP passing workflows improve by 9.07 points (95% CI 5.66–13.07)
    - number: LeanEvolve adds 7.47 points on SWE, raising 56.93% to 64.40%
    - number: formal-guided evolution solves 7.00 points more initially failed ELAIP cases than pure-LLM evolve
    - limits: Layer-2 soundness depends on LLMExec: each step satisfying predicates is assumed to produce postconditions; this abstracts away failures of long-horizon black-box LLM behavior. Custom predicates may have only name existence and natural-language explanations, and trajectory checks can rely on an LLM judge. Evaluation uses small random subsets (50 SWE and 100 ELAIP), 40 generated workflows and contemporary model versions, so generalization and causal effects are limited. The paper is a preprint and reports no mechanized verification of the LLM internals or distributed-system protocols.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06523); [notes: ## Lean4Agent (2026)](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `lean4agent-formalagentlib-and-leanevolve-2026`

    - title: Evaluating LLM-Generated ACSL Annotations for Formal Verification
    - what: Empirically compares a rule-based Python annotator, Frama-C RTE, and DeepSeek-V3.2, GPT-5.2 and OLMo 3.1 32B for one-shot ACSL generation on filtered CASP C programs, checking annotations through Frama-C WP and multiple SMT solvers. Uses 355 retained programs from the 506-file CASP dataset, evaluates EVA preprocessing and hundreds of WP runs per source/solver, and reports proof success, solver timeouts and Qed time. It finds rule-based and RTE annotations more reliable; DeepSeek is the strongest LLM but remains variable and can produce weak contracts.

Results vary substantially by solver and do not establish generalization beyond this benchmark.

- LORIS loop invariant synthesis (2026)
    - title: Guiding LLM-based Loop Invariant Synthesis via Feedback on Local Reasoning Errors
    - what: LORIS iteratively synthesizes C loop invariants: an LLM proposes an invariant, writes a natural-language proof of failed verification conditions, another LLM translates local steps into first-order implications, and Z3 checks those implications to produce targeted feedback. The framework checks local reasoning steps rather than demanding a fully formal proof script, maps an invalid implication back to a specific natural-language error, and feeds that diagnosis into subsequent invariant synthesis. It integrates Frama-C for inductiveness, Z3 for implication validity, and evaluates multiple model families against LaM4Inv and Clause2Inv, including a BMC combination study and manual auto-formalization checks.
    - number: GPT-4.1 LORIS solves 445/460 main programs with 93.1% success, versus 84.6% and 84.9% baseline success rates
    - number: best reported non-linear result is 47/50 programs; GPT-4.1 LORIS solves 45/50 at 83.2% success
    - number: five runs produce 2,300 attempts on the main suite; per-run cap is 150,000 tokens and 600 seconds
    - number: successful GPT-4.1 run costs about $0.038 on average; sampled auto-formalization evaluates 108 proof steps
    - limits: Results use single-loop, single-function C programs filtered from established suites; floating-point cases unsupported by the chosen Frama-C version were removed. Each method receives five independent runs, a 600-second limit, and 150,000 tokens per run; success means the verifier accepts the generated invariant. Feedback quality depends on LLM formalization of reasoning steps and solver-supported first-order implications, so it does not establish correctness of arbitrary natural-language reasoning outside the checked obligations.

    - title: MINI F2F-DAFNY: LLM-Guided Mathematical Theorem Proving via Auto-Active Verification
    - what: Translates the miniF2F mathematical benchmark into Dafny and measures how SMT automation plus LLM-generated proof hints perform in an auto-active verifier. The benchmark has 244 test and 244 validation lemmas with compact definitions.dfy and library.dfy support. Dafny translates Hoare-style specifications through Boogie weakest preconditions to Z3; ghost assertions, calc chains and lemmas guide opaque solver search. The authors validate that generated solutions preserve requires/ensures, reject assume or axiom cheating, and audit the trusted support layer: 128 of 160 originally axiomatized lemmas are proved, 14 selected lemmas are independently solved, and 142 of 201 empty-proof successes remain without definitions.dfy. LLM attempts receive up to four trajectories and three error-correction rounds.
    - number: 488 total problems
    - number: 244 test / 244 validation
    - number: 38.9% empty-proof test baseline
    - number: 62.7% Claude Opus pass@4
    - limits: The translation is human-led and the mathematical support is an intentionally small axiomatized library rather than a foundational Mathlib formalization; opaque transcendental and number-theoretic properties remain trusted. Results use Dafny 4.11/Z3, 30-second attempts, eight off-the-shelf models, and no fine-tuning. Verification is brittle to assertion order/calc organization; models often confuse Dafny syntax with Lean and fail when required facts are absent. Pass rates measure generated Dafny code accepted by the validator, not independent proof objects.

- Neuro-formal verification (2026)
    - title: Neuro-Formal Verification: Agentic Language-Agnostic Formal Program Reasoning
    - what: Proposes NFV: an AI agent translates mainstream-language source, property and environment assumptions into staged formal artifacts, then uses Dafny or CBMC to produce machine-checked proofs/refutations. Goal-blind stages (formalize model, property and environment separately before proof search) reduce proving a mismatched artifact, while admitting formalization itself is not soundly guaranteed. The pipeline distinguishes verification and refutation arms, performs artifact audits and abstains when formalization/type checks fail. A balanced nl2post-safe corpus has 206 program/spec pairs (103 correct, 103 mutants), with library tiers L0–L3. Dafny handles proof/refutation; CBMC translates libraries to C for bounded counterexamples. Error analysis attributes wrong verdicts to missing or stronger preconditions and library formalization.
    - number: 206 entries: 103 correct + 103 buggy
    - number: Dafny pipeline issued 128 verdicts, 10 wrong: 92.2% precision, 57.3% recall, F1 0.71
    - number: Dafny verification 72/103 correct (93.5% precision); refutation 47/103 buggy (88.7% precision)
    - number: CBMC refuted 65/103 buggy, 7 false: 63% recall, 90% precision
    - limits: NFV optimizes empirical accuracy rather than end-to-end soundness because informal environment assumptions cannot be fully guaranteed. Results assume docstrings capture intended properties; unstated preconditions caused all six false refutations. Dafny modeling of libraries and Python semantics is incomplete; CBMC is bounded and checks C translation rather than original Python.

    - title: Toward Guarantees for Clinical Reasoning in Vision Language Models via Formal Verification
    - what: Introduces a reference-free neurosymbolic audit that maps VLM Findings and Impression text into propositional evidence, checks diagnostic entailment with a clinician-audited knowledge base and Z3, and classifies claims as supported, hallucinated, missed, or correctly excluded. The method formalizes an assume-guarantee contract around visual grounding and text-to-symbolic translation, uses a closed-world binary evidence vector, and reports soundness/completeness alongside precision, recall, F1 and specificity. Seven VLMs are tested on five chest-X-ray datasets; symbolic filtering is then compared with raw impressions on CheXpert and NIH-CXR14, showing a consistent precision/soundness gain with a small completeness/recall trade-off.

- Numina-Lean-Agent (2026)
    - title: Numina-Lean-Agent: An Open and General Agentic Reasoning System for Formal Mathematics
    - what: Combines a general coding agent (Claude Code with Opus4.5) and Numina-Lean-MCP tools to perform formal mathematics with autonomous tool selection. Reports12/12 Putnam2025 formal problems solved,matching AxiomProver,and a human-assisted harmonic-analysis formalization exceeding8,000 Lean lines in under two weeks. The contribution is agent/tool integration and case evidence,not a new model-training algorithm or mechanized correctness theorem for the agent. Four tool groups:Lean-LSP diagnostics/goals/search/code;LeanDex semantic theorem retrieval;informal generator/verifier;discussion partner for alternative reasoning. LeanDex rebuilds Mathlib/FLT/PhysLean under Lean4.26.0,regenerates descriptions using DeepSeekChat3.1,optimizes queries and reranks results with LLMs,with claimed monthly updates. Gemini3ProPreview generator/verifier refines up to20 times and requires three unanimous informal checks;these guide search rather than certify proofs. Subagents isolate lemmas in temporary files and merge successful verified lemmas,executed sequentially with main-agent blocking. For sustained research formalization,a dependency DAG blueprint records definitions/intermediate lemmas/final theorem and is revised from Lean feedback,human hints and external discussion.
    - number: 12 of 12 Putnam 2025 problems solved, budget about $50 per problem, about $300 for B6 and about $1000 for A5
    - number: A5 took 2040 minutes and 3263 lines of Lean; B1 took 55 minutes and 328 lines
    - number: Brascamp-Lieb: more than 8,000 lines of Lean in under two weeks, about 70 new definitions, lemmas and theorems added by the agent
    - number: On B4, iterative refinement of informal proofs finished in 5 rounds; independent sampling failed in 10 rounds
    - limits: Formal-checking trust is relative to Lean,the exact formal statement,imported libraries and harness. Putnam uses Seed-Prover1.5 formal statements and disabled online API search,but existing library retrieval is enabled. No artifact or proof certificate was independently checked by this reader;supplied AppendixA statement ends by sorry and AppendixB lemma has no proof body. Twelve Putnam problems plus selected six-problem ablations are small evaluations. No multiple-run variance,random seeds,confidence intervals or standardized hardware/API latency accounting is provided. Inter-system timing/line comparisons are reported values,not demonstrated matched-compute causal comparisons. Default budget approximately$50/problem is raised to$1000 A5 and$300 B6;authors call these relative effort rather than exact accounting. Main headline is success under unequal per-problem resources,not fixed-cost12/12. Sequential execution avoids parallel search but does not imply uniform cost or faster overall solving. The research case is collaborative:one mathematician,one Lean expert and agent use human intent review,inline hints,priorities,type conversions and blueprint edits. >8,000 lines and≈70 new objects are output size,not a human-effort reduction estimate. No human-only control,total token count or complete task-time accounting is reported. LLM informal verification and discussion are fallible search guidance. Three agreeing checks by the same model family are not independent formal certificates. Lean diagnoses formal acceptance;human review is still needed when the agent changes mathematical assumptions or theorem statements. Tool ablations couple informal proving and discussion;the reported triples do not isolate each tool’s causal contribution. AppendixD separately claims A5/B6 fail without discussion,but supplies no corresponding numeric table,budget or repeated-run data. Invocation frequency alone cannot establish necessity. Base-model ablation has Opus6/6,DeepSeekV3.2 3/6,KimiK2 zero shown successes on six problems. Openness/interchangeability is an architectural claim;published tools still rely on proprietary Claude/Gemini components in best configuration. No Rust/Verus/TLA+ or distributed-system verification evaluation.

    - title: From Execution to Necessity: Proof-Based Metrics for Code Coverage (Short Paper)
    - what: Introduces proof-based coverage: translate a C program with an assertion to SSA and an SMT formula, extract an unsatisfiable core proving the assertion, and map core constraints back to source statements. Proof line coverage measures statements necessary for validity rather than merely executed. Defines Trace(P) and Proof(P), line coverage LC(P)=|Trace(P)|/|N(P)| and proof line coverage PrLC(P,T)=|Proof(P)|/|N(P)|. The prototype translates a restricted loop-free C subset to SSA/SMT and marks a minimal unsat core. The paper adds non-trace conditions using undefined-variable metadata, states Lemma 1 and Theorem 1 that proof statements share one execution trace, and derives PrLC≤LC. A Siemens TCAS experiment found lines repeatedly execution-covered but never proof-covered; replacing the relevant if with result=0 left all tests outputs unchanged.
    - number: TCAS source about 75 lines
    - number: about 500 TCAS test cases
    - number: fan example LC=11/15≈0.69
    - limits: Integer arithmetic approximates machine arithmetic; language subset omits loops and translation details. Minimal unsat cores are assumed and the solver is assumed deterministic despite generally returning non-minimal/multiple cores. Current encoding does not model short-circuit evaluation (sound but coverage may change), and scaling to larger datasets may be problematic. Theorem 1/PrLC≤LC rely on added non-trace conditions.

- Reality Is the Final Verifier: On Two Key Gaps in Agentic Software Engineering (2026)
    - what: Argues that verification stops short in two places. The written requirements may not match what the stakeholder wants (the requirement gap). The model used to evaluate code may not match the real environment (the model gap). Proposes an inner loop, which revises code against the requirements, the environment model and the evaluator. Proposes an outer loop, which uses observations from deployment to revise those inputs.

- Scimitar proof decomposition (2026)
    - title: Interactive Safety Verification of Distributed Protocols by Inductive Proof Decomposition
    - what: Introduces inductive proof decomposition and the Scimitar tool for interactively constructing compositional safety proofs. An inductive proof graph decomposes lemmas by protocol actions; counterexample traces are localized and variable slicing hides irrelevant state. Scimitar combines TLC and Apalache to generate counterexamples to inductive proof obligations and TLAPS to validate final proof graphs. The user works backward from a target property, adding support edges and local lemmas; static slicing presents only variables relevant to each node. Five TLA+ protocols were proved, including industrial-scale AsyncRaft with asynchronous messaging and fine-grained local state. The authors report the first invariant effort at that Raft complexity and inspect proof-graph structure as a correctness insight.
    - number: 5 protocol specifications
    - number: AsyncRaft: 12 variables, 11 actions, 638 spec LOC/920 proof LOC, 39 lemmas, median slice 5 variables (0.42)
    - number: AsyncRaft inductive invariant developed in approximately 3 human-weeks
    - number: Other reported proof-development burdens: 1–2 human-months for smaller invariants
    - limits: TLC/Apalache counterexamples use finite parameter bounds and do not themselves prove arbitrary-size invariants; TLAPS validation is required for the final proof. Human guidance remains necessary to add lemmas and edges. Evaluation is on five TLA+ specifications and one implementation; generality to other formalisms is untested. AsyncRaft development took human effort despite automation.

- SL-VC and SPLIT (2026)
    - title: SL-VC: A Benchmark and Automated Framework for Separation Logic Verification Condition Proving
    - what: Introduces SL-VC, 289 separation-logic verification conditions from textbook data structures and real C system code, and SPLIT, an LLM-friendly Rocq tactic library plus two-stage spatial/pure proof workflow. SPLIT decomposes mixed entailments into spatial elimination and independent pure goals, using explicit tactics such as split_pure_spatial, cancel, sep_apply and pure_solve with verifier-driven repair. The benchmark includes linked lists, merge sort, trees, LiteOS circular lists and mini-gmp; the full 288-VC evaluation and 207-VC core subset separate benchmark construction from prover comparisons. CoqHammer is evaluated as an alternative pure-stage solver, and failures concentrate in arithmetic-heavy mini-gmp and recurrence-like goals.

Pass rates measure verified scripts under finite repair budgets, not complete verification of whole C systems.

- Spec-Harness (formerly VeriAct) (2026)
    - title: VeriAct / Spec-Harness: Beyond Verifiability — Agentic Synthesis of Correct and Complete Formal Specifications
    - what: # Spec-Harness: Assessing and Guiding LLM-Synthesized Formal Specifications Beyond Verifiability (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Md Rakib Hossain Misu, Cristina V. Lopes, Iris Ma, Yuangang Li and Shuvendu Lahiri, arXiv 2604.00280v3, 9 October 2026. This empirical study separates verifier acceptance from how well a generated contract describes behavior, then supplies four test-based scores to specification-writing agents. It reports stronger postconditions on held-out tests, with extra time and sometimes fewer verified tasks. These are finite empirical adequacy results, not proofs that generated contracts capture every intended behavior (Q0,Q14,Q17).  ## Reading and evidence  Read all 22 pages, sections 1–9, data availability and all 49 reference entries. Inspected all six figures, five tables and eleven numbered equations; viewed individual renders of pages 4–18. Official and inventory PDFs are byte-identical. No appendix appears. Reference entries were read but their papers were not recursively followed. The placeholder journal header is not treated as an identified peer-reviewed publication.  The paper links Mondego/vACT. Inspected its metadata, complete nontruncated tree and fifteen acquired text/code blobs at commit 5b158553a05c38c39b3a029e38a99b020eb203c6, dated 29 June 2026. Four READMEs and the small configuration file were read completely; source-scope.json lists selected code ranges. The repository README describes the earlier title “VeriAct: Beyond Verifiability -- Agentic Synthesis of Correct and Complete Formal Specifications” (C0). The pinned source predates the October paper and differs materially from its method. No experiment results were reproduced or independently attributed to this commit. No author code was executed/imported, no compiler/verifier/solver was run and no serialized benchmark, model or experiment was loaded.  ## What the method measures  The motivating changeCase contract restricts inputs to A through z and promises only an uppercase-range result for lowercase inputs. It excludes legitimate punctuation inputs and leaves the exact output underconstrained. The desired repairs go in opposite directions: weaken the precondition and strengthen the postcondition. The original, empty and exact specifications get reported mutation completeness rates of 0.36, 0.09 and 0.94. Of 33 code mutants, two are equivalent; some failures arise from a range check rather than the contract. A single mutation-kill score therefore neither isolates the defective clause nor proves that every failed verification detected a wrong output (Q1).  Spec-Harness instead checks small harness programs with fixed inputs and outputs. Let ψ be the precondition and φ the postcondition. Postcondition checks use ψ⇒φ, with each JML specification case guarded by its own precondition. Precondition checks additionally include implicit non-null requirements from the stated verifier configuration. This guard makes a rejected valid input vacuously pass the postcondition check; the separate precondition metric is essential (Q7–Q9).  PostCorr is the fraction of decided valid input/output checks satisfying ψ⇒φ. PostComp is the fraction of decided mutated-output checks satisfying ¬(ψ⇒φ), requiring both an admitted input and a rejected wrong output. PreCorr is the fraction of decided normally returning inputs admitted by the effective precondition. PreComp is the fraction of decided failing inputs rejected by it. Thus true postconditions can have perfect PostCorr and zero PostComp; false preconditions can have perfect PostCorr/PreComp but fail PreCorr/PostComp. These are complementary finite-test scores, not universal correctness/completeness theorems (Q7–Q9; reader examples).  For each concrete check, the paper describes two parallel proof obligations, one for the predicate and one for its negation. Neither proving within 300 seconds makes the check undecided and removes it from the within-specification denominator. With contracted helpers, exactly one must prove; this guards against inconsistent helper assumptions proving both. The verdict is a proof about the generated stub under its encoding and verifier assumptions. It is not a proof about all inputs to the original method (Q7).  For benchmark aggregation, a specification must also verify against the original method. Missing postconditions and changed code receive zero on all four metrics and remain in aggregate denominators. Pure helpers, annotations and braces around a single statement are permitted edits. A failed synthesis or a metric with no decided checks does not reach any positive threshold. PreComp denominators include only tasks with known failing inputs. PostHarness@τ is the fraction of all synthesis attempts whose PostCorr and PostComp both reach τ; it is distinct from the conditional share among only verified outputs (Q11).  Input generation combines EvoSuite, parameter boundaries and inputs killing additional Major code mutants. The unchanged reference implementation/test driver labels normal returns as valid and exceptions/timeouts as candidate invalid inputs. Pools of at least 100 pairs are divided into disjoint feedback/evaluation lists, ranked by added branch and mutation coverage; five evaluation pairs are scored. Up to five deterministic output perturbations are intended, all different from the reference output. This assumes the reference behavior is an adequate oracle. A reference bug or a permissible alternative outcome is not ruled out merely by matching one recorded execution (Q10; reader oracle qualification).  The first five pairs reportedly cover 98.7%/97.9% of branches and kill 89.1%/89.3% of Major mutants on SpecGenBench/FormalBench, versus 96.7%/92.3% and 81.3%/76.9% for shipped examples. Evaluation-side invalid inputs average 3.4/5.6 per task; 55/146 tasks have none. Array3d is excluded for unparsable output. High coverage of selected branch/mutant sets does not establish adequacy over untested behavior (Q10).  ## Experiments and reported findings  RQ1 compares Houdini, Daikon and eleven prompt configurations of SpecGen, AutoSpec and FormalBench using six models. The datasets have 120 SpecGenBench methods and 662 filtered FormalBench methods, drawn from 700 after excluding void/Object cases. Class/method names are normalized to Solution/solve. This evaluates reconstructing behavior from implementation, not formalizing independent natural-language intent. Void methods can in general have meaningful heap postconditions; excluding them limits this result rather than establishing that such contracts cannot be evaluated (Q2; reader qualification).  The six models are GPT-4o (2024-08-06), Gemini 2.0 Flash, Claude Sonnet 4.6, Qwen2.5-Coder-32B, DeepSeek-Coder-33B and CodeLlama-34B. Prompt runs use temperature 0.7, up to ten SpecGen/AutoSpec refinement rounds versus three for FormalBench. There are 132 prompt/model/benchmark configurations plus four classical runs. OpenJML 21-0.21, Java 21.0.4, cvc4, non-null defaults, quiet arithmetic failures and a 300-second limit are stated. Sixty-four outputs with internal errors are excluded despite a verified indication (Q2,Q3).  Table 2 reports Houdini verifying 113/120 and 443/662 tasks, Daikon 34/120 and 159/662. Best prompt-based results are SpecGen 82/120 (68.3%), FormalBench prompts 249/662 (37.6%); AutoSpec's FormalBench best in this table is 200/662 (30.2%). Figure 3 nevertheless prints 0.39 for AutoSpec zero-shot/Qwen on FormalBench. Section 5.8 explains that configuration verifies 255 unannotated outputs (38.5%) and Figure 5 selects the best configuration among outputs containing JML. Table 2's “Best” heading and RQ1 prose do not clearly state that same exclusion. Preserve these distinct selection rules instead of reporting 30.2% as the unrestricted maximum (Q3,Q4,Q14; reader cross-reference).  Across 14,936 verified outputs, 3,395 (22.7%) lack postconditions, 1,888 lack JML entirely, 840 (5.6%) change code and 657 alter the method body. The union of no-postcondition/changed-code outputs is 3,839 (25.7%), so categories overlap; summing their percentages would double-count. Table 2's full per-approach counts and Figure 3's two heatmaps are retained in numeric-evidence.json (Q3,Q4).  RQ2 uses GEPA/DSPy with GPT-4o as generator and reflection model, a medium budget and a stratified FormalBench split of 101/50/511 train/validation/test methods. Two SpecGenBench few-shot examples are removed from its 118-task evaluation. The numeric reward is 1 for verification, 0.3 for one verification error without syntax errors, 0.1 for multiple verification errors and zero for syntax errors/empty output; classified feedback also guides revision (Q5).  Table 3's seed→optimized rates are: zero-shot 39.83→46.61% on SpecGenBench and 17.81→18.59% on FormalBench; few-shot chain-of-thought 46.61→44.07% and 19.18→19.77%; least-to-most unchanged at 45.76%/19.96%. No optimized prompt beats the best seed on either dataset. This finite experiment does not prove an inherent ceiling for verifier feedback, other optimizers, models or budgets (Q6; reader inference boundary).  RQ3 scores 14,934 verified specifications after excluding two Array3d outputs. It does not reevaluate optimized prompts. Only 65.2% reach PostCorr≥0.75, 40.1% reach PostComp≥0.75 and 35.6% reach both; these percentages condition on verified outputs. Eighteen-point-nine percent have perfect PostCorr but PostComp below 0.25. Houdini's aggregate PostHarness@0.75 falls to about 2%/0%; 90.7% of its verified outputs with postconditions reject every valid tested input. Daikon often accepts observed outputs without discriminating wrong ones: 45.6% have perfect PostCorr and PostComp<0.25. Among verified prompt outputs, FormalBench prompts reach both thresholds on 64.4%/42.1%, versus SpecGen 44.0%/28.9% and AutoSpec 41.1%/25.3% (Q13,Q14).  The instrument is imperfect. On 360 sampled specifications, five versus twelve evaluation pairs change threshold attainment by 2.1/1.6 percentage points on average and at most 5.6 points. The authors treat differences up to roughly six points as measurement resolution. Two blinded senior PhD raters assess 100 specifications and 64 individual checks. Before discussion, κ is 0.93 for completeness, 0.75 for adequacy and 0.94 for individual checks. Against consensus, PreCorr/PreComp κ are 0.72/0.70 and PostComp's completeness correlation is ρ=0.64. PostHarness@0.75 agrees on adequacy for only 67/100 specifications (Q12).  On the 55 of those 100 specifications for which FormalBench CR is defined, CR correlates with human completeness more strongly than PostComp (ρ=0.72 versus 0.45). Only 27% of CR detections are postcondition rejections, versus 32% timeouts and 40% other verification failures; rounded percentages sum to 99%. The study supports more interpretable clause feedback, not uniformly superior correlation with expert judgment (Q12).  RQ4 runs Claude Code 2.1.284/Sonnet 4.6, Codex 0.159.2/gpt-6.1-sol and VeriAct/gpt-6.1-sol at medium reasoning effort. Each gets ten tool calls, with or without the harness, on 120 tasks from each benchmark. Final evaluation uses disjoint held-out pairs and requires verification, a postcondition and unchanged code. Each agent runs once per task. The 12 agent/condition/benchmark combinations imply 1,440 task sessions; the paper's 720 sessions receiving harness feedback are the six with-harness combinations (Q15–Q18; reader accounting).  PostHarness@0.75 increases on SpecGenBench from 0.52→0.62 for Claude Code, 0.60→0.75 for Codex and 0.57→0.63 for VeriAct. On FormalBench-120 it goes 0.23→0.28, 0.25→0.41 and 0.25→0.28. Exact McNemar tests find significance for Codex on both datasets and Claude Code on SpecGenBench (p≤0.005), but not the other three comparisons (p=0.18–0.45). Small gains also sit near the instrument's stated resolution; significance on the sampled task labels does not remove uncertainty about broader adequacy (Q12,Q17; reader qualification).  Verification can worsen: Claude Code drops 0.86→0.74 and 0.68→0.45, Codex 0.88→0.88 and 0.74→0.69, VeriAct 0.90→0.88 and 0.87→0.68. Thirty-seven Claude Code tasks meet the harness target without verifying. Harness runs use 1.3–2.3 harness calls per task and take 1.4–2.2 times as long. Table 5 preserves all thresholds and precondition scores; at threshold1, VeriAct on FormalBench-120 drops 0.24→0.23. Neither universal improvement on every metric nor a free speedup follows (Q17).  ## Static artifact comparison  The acquired June source implements an earlier scorer. The following are explicit differences from the October description, not evidence that the reported October experiments used this source (C0–C9).  The selected runner classifies generic warning/verification-failure/error text as FAIL. Pair evaluation credits FAIL as a killed output or rejected input, using one obligation rather than a proof of the negated predicate. Aggregation divides by all recorded details, retaining unknown checks; no exactly-one-of-two-helper-proof gate appears in these paths. The earlier standalone SpecHarness class behaves similarly. These paths cannot independently substantiate the later paper's “every verdict is a proof” implementation claim (C1,C2,C6,C9).  The selected scalar mutator does not remove unchanged outputs: for an integer result0 its printed candidate list is [1,−1,2,−2,0]. String/float/map cases can likewise retain unchanged values or duplicates; arrays explicitly filter them. An exact contract for the correct result cannot reject that unchanged value. This differs from the paper's “no mutant equals” requirement; it is a direct static counterexample, not an observed run (C3,Q8).  The earlier input generator labels null, empty, singleton and −1 variants invalid without running the reference to confirm failure in this function. The selected evaluate_problem calls that generator directly. A negative integer need not be invalid, for example for absolute value. This differs from the paper's confirmed-failure test construction; the final October labeling pipeline was not acquired or verified (C4,C6,Q9,Q10).  The selected post/pre stubs declare nullable_by_default, while the command also supplies nonnull-by-default. That source does not straightforwardly establish the October effective-precondition account. Precondition stubs include and call the submitted annotated code, so they are not the stated body-free predicate stubs. These are encoding differences; no OpenJML interpretation or diagnostic was replayed (C5,C1,Q7).  In the inspected external-agent CLI paths, verify sends the submitted code to OpenJML and harness reads its annotations; neither compares it with the original body. submit derives its passed flag from saved harness scores, without requiring a successful verification of the final code or binding those scores to its content. The scaffold writes the same five visible pairs into tests/ and task.json; a separate held-out split is not implemented in that path. Other offline selection/scoring could impose the paper's rules, but it was not audited or established here (C7,C8,Q11,Q15–Q17).  The verifier wrapper also defaults to 180 seconds rather than the paper's 300-second configuration. The standalone scorer/agent scorer use different defaults, and READMEs describe earlier model/tool setups. Pin, version and actual experiment configuration must therefore remain separate. All fifteen blob identities are verified against the pinned Git tree; that proves which bytes were read, not that they generated the paper's measurements (C0,C10).  ## Limits and relevance  The paper excludes frame conditions and exceptional postconditions, cannot express in-place array updates in the stubs, and acknowledges overflow/rounding and small-output-domain limitations. Its finite pairs, chosen mutants, reference implementation, verifier semantics and helper contracts bound every score. Removing undecided checks prevents treating solver failure as falsity but can change which cases/specifications contribute; a high decided-case score should be reported with decided coverage. A contradiction in assumptions can also make individual stub proofs vacuous unless excluded. These are reasons to retain the score components and coverage rather than treat them as a correctness certificate (Q7–Q12,Q18; reader qualification).  This paper directly informs evaluation of LLM-generated specifications: code preservation, vacuity, separate input/output obligations, failure versus refutation, held-out feedback and verification/adequacy/cost tradeoffs. Its study is Java/JML, not distributed-protocol, TLA+ or Verus verification. Verus/Rust and Frama-C ports are proposed future work. For distributed proof agents, analogous checks would require protocol execution oracles, message/schedule coverage and relational safety/liveness properties; transferring the four method-contract scores unchanged is a reader proposal, not a demonstrated result (Q19,Q20).
    - number: v2 uses thresholds such as PostCorr >= 0.75 and PostComp >= 0.75 (operational choice)

    - number: SpecGen falls from 54% verifier acceptance to 24% meaningfully verified on SpecGenBench (§5.4)
    - number: three refinement cycles; improvement of 5 points on SpecGenBench and 12 on FormalBench (§6.1)
    - limits: Finitebehavioraladequacy;referenceoracle/verifier/encoding/testselection;singlerun;coverageandunknownverdicts;JuneartifactnotestablishedOctexperimentrevision
    - how much was read: most of it read

- SpecLoop (2026)
    - title: SpecLoop: An Agentic RTL-to-Specification Framework with Formal Verification Feedback Loop
    - what: Generates natural-language RTL specifications, reconstructs RTL from them, and uses formal equivalence counterexamples to iteratively refine specifications. The verifier separates compilation, reconstruction and equivalence errors; pass/fail-only and full-diagnosis loops are compared with single-round generation. Feedback is withheld from the reconstructor to avoid leaking original RTL semantics. VerilogEval and RTLLM benchmarks are scored by RTL Reconstruction (RR), making specifications operationally testable.
    - number: Verified specs have RR=1 ratios 81.3–91%; unverified 34.6–45% across settings
    - number: Verification loop ranks first in 4/12 (pass/fail) and 9/12 (full diagnosis) settings vs single round
    - number: Qualitative reset case corrected after equivalence counterexample
    - limits: End-to-end results depend on reconstructor quality, FEC resource bounds and heuristic feedback parsing. RR uses testbenches in addition to formal equivalence and may not capture all semantics. The method stops on invalid original RTL or exhausted retries.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02895); [notes: SpecLoop](../distributed_verification_primary_extension_20261012.md)
    - record id: `specloop-2026`
- SpecSyn (2026)
    - title: SpecSyn: LLM-based Synthesis and Refinement of Formal Specifications for Real-world Program Verification
    - what: SpecSyn combines top-down AST/dependency decomposition and sketch planning with bottom-up ACSL generation, verifier-guided repair, and variant-discrimination refinement. Semantic-non-equivalent mutations test whether candidate specifications reject changed programs; undistinguished variants are fed back for stronger specifications. Tarjan SCCs produce dependency-respecting segments, and depth-first postorder handles nested POIs such as loop invariants and function postconditions. At each POI, Frama-C/WP verifies generated candidates, returns errors for repair, and deduplicates accepted specifications. Variant discriminative rate measures the fraction of 188 mutation operators (35 equivalent operators removed; GCC -O2 TCE filters equivalents) rejected by the specification set. Experiments use 50 real-world C programs, seven verification benchmarks totaling 1,421 targets, three LLMs, and 17 trained reviewers.
    - number: GPT-5 SpecSyn average precision 96.68%, recall 75.91% across 14 repositories
    - number: SpecSyn verifies 1,022/1,421 targets vs Codex 883, Preguss 510, AutoSpec 259
    - number: Ablating decomposition lowers precision/recall by 19.39/15.72 points; refinement by 11.39/12.66; sketch by 2.05/5.34
    - number: 17 reviewers rated SpecSyn 3.78 (understanding) and 4.07 (semantic accuracy) out of 5
    - limits: Semantic strength is operationalized by a finite mutation set and may miss behaviors not exercised by mutations. Precision/recall use manually authored ground truth, which may omit valid alternative specifications; Frama-C/WP and Z3 incompleteness can reject correct statements. The method is computationally expensive, with hundreds of verifier calls per file, and benchmark scale is 50 programs with potential leakage from prior X509 data. ACSL-compatible deterministic C functions are selected, excluding many OS-dependent functions.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21570)
    - record id: `specsyn-2026`
- Specula (2026)
    - title: Specula: Scaling formal specifications for autonomous model checking of system code
    - what: Specula writes TLA+ models and properties from system source code. It checks observed traces against the model and repairs disagreements. It then model checks for violations and tries to reproduce each one in the real code.
    - number: 48 open-source systems; 249 reproduced bugs; 89 reported to maintainers; 68 confirmed and 24 fixed in that subset
    - number: only 0.90% of generated properties are liveness; 99.10% safety
    - number: 200 bugs via model checking, 49 during code reading/modeling
    - number: median 3.69 hours and $57 per system across 48 systems (§5.3)
    - limits: Model checking plus reproduction gives concrete failures, not proofs for all executions. Its coverage of liveness is weak. The results pool several versions, and only 14 systems used the latest version.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25333); [notes: Specula: close the model–code loop before calling a violation a bug](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_5_llm_agents.md)

- TESTDAFNY110 (2026)
    - title: Automatic Generation of Formal Specification and Verification Annotations Using LLMs and Test Oracles
    - what: # Whole-paper read: Automatic Generation of Formal Specification and Verification Annotations Using LLMs and Test Oracles (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  João Pascoal Faria, Emanuel Trigo, Vinicius Honorato, Rui Abreu. arXiv 2601.12845v1, 19 January 2026, 37 pages. The title-page footnote says “Preprint. Intended for submission to Science of Computer Programming.” Treat this as a preprint; the inspected version supplies no acceptance claim. [Q1, Q2]  The paper measures LLM generation of annotations for existing conventional Dafny programs: pre/postconditions, loop invariants, auxiliary predicates/functions, and assertions/lemmas. Its principal result is empirical: a Claude Opus 4.5/GPT-5.2 combination with verifier feedback solves 108/110 benchmark programs. It supplies a benchmark, prompts, repair/minimization workflow and IDE assistant. It does not prove that test-derived contracts completely express natural-language intent. The most useful contribution to evaluating proof agents is the distinction between a verifying program and an adequate contract. [Q3, Q20, Q30, Q46]  ## What is checked  The input retains implementation code, natural-language comments and outcome-checking test assertions while stripping formal annotations and helpers. Dafny 4.11.0 checks generated/postprocessed outputs with a one-minute timeout. Calls inside tests use the method contract and ignore its body: “These static test cases therefore act as specification-level oracles.” Thus a weak postcondition may allow the implementation to verify yet fail the caller’s assertion. For a tested input x and acceptable-output assertion R(y), §8.2 spells out P(x) ∧ ∀y.(Q(x, y) ⇒ R(y)). This constrains that input and permitted outputs under the generated contract. It does not establish correctness of a contract on untested inputs. Verification also depends on Dafny’s translation, SMT reasoning, trusted semantics and any retained/provided helper definitions. No new verifier-soundness theorem is given. [Q12, Q13, Q4, Q46]  The direct and repair prompts were designed iteratively, with generic patterns and recurring fixes; no separate held-out prompt-design evaluation is described. Prompts prohibit implementation changes, unproved assume statements, decreases* and removal of test assertions. Postprocessing repairs formatting/placement issues such as misplaced invariants. These are guardrails, not a demonstrated enforcement theorem: §6.5 says “generated solutions should be manually inspected.” In the inspected generator acceptance path, success follows verification; I found no mechanical comparison enforcing unchanged original implementation there. That is a limited static observation, not evidence that a reported solution cheated. [Q8, Q9, Q37, Q51, Q59]  ## Dataset and experimental protocol  TESTDAFNY110 combines 85 refined loop-based MBPP-DFY programs, 15 LOOPINV100 academic/numerical/search/sort examples, and 10 new programs intended to reduce prior exposure. Selection excludes duplicates and cases solved trivially without loops. Table 1 totals 2505 original LOC, 1211 annotation LOC and 188 proof-helper annotations across 110 programs; 56% of programs require helpers. All eight category rows are transcribed in numeric-evidence.json. This is a collection of small-to-moderate algorithmic exercises, not a distributed implementation benchmark. [Q5, Q6, Q7, Q8]  Direct prompting makes up to 5 independent attempts, stopping at success. Repair starts with a direct attempt and allows up to 9 feedback repairs, 10 attempts total. Models are Claude Opus 4.5 (claude-opus-4-5-20251101), GPT-5.2 (gpt-5.2-2025-12-11, reasoning none/low), DeepSeek-V3.2 chat and GPT-4 (gpt-4-0613). Claude is tried at temperatures 0.5 and 0; DeepSeek/GPT-4 at 0.5; the paper describes GPT-5.2 as fixed T=1. The multimodel method runs Claude T=0.5 and GPT-5.2 low in parallel each round, choosing syntax validity, verification, fewer LOC, then shorter verification time. Consequently one multimodel attempt can entail two API calls. pass@k/repair@k mean the empirical percentage of these 110 problems solved within the specified budget; no combinatorial pass@k estimator is used. [Q12, Q13, Q14, Q15]  The experiments ran on an Intel Core Ultra 7 258V @2.20 GHz, 8 cores, 32 GB, Windows 11 Pro. Table 2 gives average cost/time per API call and extra program LOC among successful attempts. These are not task-total cost or end-to-end latency. There are no reported confidence intervals or repeated complete-dataset evaluations. The initial success rates differ between direct and repair runs; the strategies also have unequal budgets, so their difference is not a paired causal estimate of feedback alone. [Q16, Q17, Q12, Q13]  ## Measured results  Section 6.1 says the multimodel approach solved “98.2% of the problems within at most 8 attempts (repair@8)”. This is 108/110; the abstract instead calls them 8 repair iterations. I use the body’s attempts, which include the initial generation. The two failures are FastModularExponentiation and PrimeFactorization, with 4 and 10 manual lemmas. The downloaded manual files contain those corresponding lemma sets; the latter file is named PrimeFactors.dfy. No independent replay was performed. [Q20, Q18, Q70, Q71]  All 14 Table 2 rows are retained. At budgets 5/10, the multimodel repair rates are 94.5%/98.2%, versus 57.3% for direct@5. Claude T=0.5 repair reaches 90.0%/96.4%; GPT-5.2 low 75.5%/89.1%; GPT-5.2 none 60.0%/70.0%; DeepSeek 62.7%/69.1%; GPT-4 30.9%/34.5%. Multimodel repair averages 7.86 US cents and 28.5 s per API call; Claude T=0.5 averages 4.41 cents and 15.8 s. DeepSeek is cheapest at 0.07 cents/call; GPT-5.2 none’s repair response time is 9.1 s. These are author-reported run prices/times, not current provider quotations. Table 2 labels the averages per API call; multimodel costs approximately sum the constituent entries, so whether its unit is a provider call or combined round remains unclear. No task-total cost is inferred. [Q17, Q22, Q15]  Repair outputs have 63.2% more total program LOC than manual counterparts. Minimization removes 58% of annotation LOC and reduces total-program overhead to 10.6%, averaging 1.4 s/line removed. Those percentages use different denominators. It greedily tries generated-segment deletions subject to verification, using source alignment and dependency information; it does not establish a globally minimum proof. Appendix B uses 10 s for deletion checks; the current downloaded implementation instead starts at 5 s and adaptively caps at 30 s. Keep snapshot defaults separate from original experiment settings. Main verifier tasks average 9.7 s, or 1.9 s excluding timeouts. [Q21, Q52, Q53, Q65, Q64, Q66]  Figure 6 pools all generation attempts, including failed repair rounds. For example, multimodel outcomes are 37% success, 32% incomplete, 25% potentially incorrect, 6% timeout and 0% syntax error. These are not eventual per-program success rates. “Incomplete” means that merging with the manual solution then verifies; the paper acknowledges this heuristic can undercount incomplete outputs. “Potentially incorrect” denotes remaining verification failure, not a demonstrated semantic bug. All five plotted distributions are preserved, including rounding. [Q24, Q25]  ## Contract quality and concrete tests  The manual contract comparison is separate from the headline solve rate. It covers all 110 programs: 108 selected first verified solutions after minimization, plus the first Claude output for each of the 2 unsolved cases. Authors judge 106/110 (96.4%) logically equivalent to expert pre/postconditions. No independent equivalence prover or inter-rater assessment is described. The four mismatches are one stronger postcondition and three weaker preconditions, all compatible with the implementation. Table 3’s nine category counts and their distinct denominators are preserved. [Q30, Q33]  BinarySearch’s found/missing/duplicate tests require a result of 1 or 2 on [1, 4, 4, 6] when searching 4. Individual duplicate indices and an unsorted input appear as expected-failure tests. LinearSearch makes the central adequacy issue concrete. Clauses a+b permit results below−1; adding c sets −1≤index<length and makes the positive tests provable. Adding d requires the first occurrence, stronger than the natural-language request for an occurrence. The positive assertion k==0||k==3 cannot reject that stronger contract. An expected-nonprovable assert k==0 can expose it. MaxDistEqual illustrates the opposite: the intended input is nonempty, but the implementation safely handles empty arrays and generated preconditions omit that restriction. CountDistinct illustrates a different quality dimension: a recursive count mirroring the loop is less declarative than set cardinality despite expressing the same result. [Q10, Q32, Q33, Q31, Q67, Q72, Q73]  After manual discovery of the four mismatches, authors add expected-failure tests and regenerate successfully for all four using Claude. This followup must not be folded into the original success rate or described as the original positive suites automatically detecting every intent mismatch. Negative tests are checked by uncommenting one at a time and rejecting the candidate if it verifies. Failure to prove such a test is not generally a semantic proof that its assertion is not implied: helpers, incomplete SMT reasoning and timeouts can also cause failure. [Q34, Q58]  A further manual inspection finds 7 of 165 failed Claude repair attempts where method bodies verify but tests fail from weak/missing postconditions. This establishes useful detections in a selected failure sample. It is not a controlled ablation of test oracles and does not measure a general defect-detection rate. [Q35]  ## Regression and contamination claims  A logistic model uses original LOC, annotation LOC, proof-helper count and configuration intercepts over 1540 program/configuration pairs. Authors report βL=−0.0735, βA=−0.0475, βH=−0.5825, all Wald p<0.001, pooled AUC=0.907 and per-configuration AUC=0.80–1.00. These are conditional associations with predictors in different raw units. They do not measure causal helper difficulty or establish generalization. The saved analysis script fits on long and predicts on the same long data before computing both AUC summaries. Thus these are in-sample discrimination measures, despite §6.3’s phrase “generalizes across models.” [Q26, Q28, Q29, Q27, Q60, Q62, Q63]  Each program appears 14 times; the paper does not describe correction for this dependence. The current artifact does contain program-clustered standard-error code and a random-intercept exploratory fit. I did not run it or inspect statistical outputs, so cannot establish whether the paper’s significance claims survive that correction. The script’s mixedlm call is a general linear mixed model, not logistic mixed regression. [Q61]  Adding subdataset indicators gives χ²(2)=0.79, p=0.67, AUC 0.907→0.908. Authors interpret this and older GPT-4’s 29.1% versus GPT-5.2 none’s 36.4% direct@5 as evidence of low contamination risk. The inference remains uncertain: a nonsignificant dataset factor with only 10 new problems cannot prove absence of exposure, while model capability and age change along with possible exposure. No copying was observed in the authors’ manual comparison, but that is not a training-data audit. [Q29, Q23, Q39, Q5]  ## IDE and user study  The assistant adds pre/postcondition and invariant commands with diagnostic feedback, retries and provider fallback. Downloaded command/prompt sources corroborate that workflow; they are a current snapshot, not established as the original study build. Seven master’s students with six weeks’ Dafny experience perform six tasks: Catalan numbers, primality, recursive minimum, heap checking, selection sort and reversal. Groups of 4 and 3 use opposite tool/manual assignments, with 15 minutes per task and one hour overall. Authors report 85.7% versus 42.1% success, 6.2 versus 11.1 minutes, and SUS 74.6/100. [Q40, Q41, Q74, Q75, Q42, Q43]  These are small-sample descriptive results without confidence intervals or significance tests. The assignments imply 21 tasks per condition, but 42.1% cannot equal an integer success count out of 21 rounded to one decimal. The study artifact’s questionnaire offers Did not try, Partial and Hybrid responses and time bins, so exclusions or a different denominator may explain it; neither the inspected protocol nor questionnaire specifies the aggregate denominator or averaging rule. Do not infer 8/19 as fact or call the reported rate erroneous. Time censoring, completion population and tool-versus-hybrid classification remain unclear. [Q42, Q43, Q76]  ## Contribution to this collection  This is direct evidence for LLM agents writing specifications and proof annotations in Dafny, with explicit repair budgets, mechanically checked test assertions, proof-helper failures and proof minimization. It supplies concrete cases where verification succeeds under an intent-mismatched contract and separates empirical verification from manual adequacy judgments. These are useful evaluation lessons for future Verus/verified-Rust or distributed-system proof agents, but that transfer is my inference. The paper has no Verus, Rust, TLA+ or distributed-system experiment. Related-work comparisons, including 87.5% pass@5 versus 100% repair@6 on 64 overlapping tasks, change models, budgets and helper permissions; they are attributed comparisons, not controlled superiority experiments. The claim of loop invariants for all 110 programs also differs from full annotation success for 108. [Q3, Q30, Q47, Q44, Q48, Q49]  ## Read and source scope  Read all 37 pages, §§1–9, acknowledgments, AI declaration, both Appendix A prompts, Appendix B algorithm and 37 references. Inspected 37 page renders/19 contact sheets, including 11 figures and 3 tables. Read primary artifact sources statically at the scope recorded in source-scope.json, including all manual examples above, the entire regression script and study-artifact text; larger generator/minimizer/IDE files were inspected in selected ranges. Downloads are pinned by blob/tree identities and hashes with fetch dates. The current snapshot is not asserted to be the exact experiment checkout. No author code, compiler, checker, prover or solver was executed; no experiment spreadsheets, serialized results or models were loaded. Exact literal excerpts with section, page, line and character anchors are in quotes.json and combined.quote-source.txt. Reader deductions and author measurements remain separate in reader-diagnostics.json and numeric-evidence.json.
    - number: 98.2% of problems within at most 8 attempts
    - number: 106 of 110 (96.4%) logically equivalent to expert specs
    - number: seven-student crossover study: 85.7% vs 42.1% success, 6.2 vs 11.1 minutes (the notes call these assisted/manual success and average task minutes)
    - number: 98.2% on 110 Dafny programs within 8 repair iterations
    - limits: Existing conventional Dafny implementations, natural-language comments, chosen static positive/negative tests, Dafny 4.11.0, SMT reasoning, helpers and timeouts. Fixed 110 algorithmic programs; modular tests constrain selected inputs, not globally complete intent. Manual equivalence judgment, in-sample AUC and unclear user-study denominators. No distributed/Verus/Rust/TLA+ experiment.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12845); [notes: Automatic Generation of Formal Specification and Verification Annotati](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_12_second_pass_intent_eval.md)
    - record id: `testdafny110-2026`

- TLA+-Bench (2026)
    - title: TLA+-Bench: An Execution-Grounded Benchmark and Dataset for Natural-Language to TLA+ Specification Generation
    - what: TLA-Bench has TLA+ specs, each with a model-checker config and English descriptions. A model reads the description and writes a spec. TLC (the TLA+ model checker) runs it against the reference config. A pass means the spec parses and no listed property is violated.
    - number: 403 model-checked gold and 897 parse-only silver specs from 13 public repositories
    - number: on one fixed set of 300 outputs the correct rate moves from 10.0% to 1.7% depending on grading choices; 18.7% to 1.7% if the model is told the config's names
    - number: best model is correct 16% of the time by default and 26% given the interface names; open models at most 1%; best model parses 87%
    - number: 12 of 30 passes meet the non-vacuity bar, so the plain correct rate overstates capability about 2.5 times
    - limits: The descriptions are written by a model, not by practitioners. Composition and refinement are under-represented. The paper makes no claim that controls for contamination. A pass is exact only relative to the reference config. It does not prove that the spec reproduces the reference behavior. The notes' later pass says the benchmark creators wrote the descriptions and the grading oracle.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23425); [notes: TLA+-Bench](../distributed_verification_primary_extension_20261012.md)

    - what: Extends the standard machine-readable proof format that program verifiers hand to each other so it can carry termination proofs. The proof is a transition invariant: a relation between any earlier and later state of a run that must decrease in a well-founded way. Three checkers re-validate such proofs independently of the tool that made them.

One reduction route needs finite state spaces and TransVer has limited support for arrays and structs. Witnesses with many invariants (Goblint) often could not be validated within limits. Not about fairness or liveness of concurrent or distributed systems.

- Unit proofs study (2026)
    - title: Do Unit Proofs Work? An Empirical Study of Compositional Bounded Model Checking for Memory Safety Verification
    - what: Studies a systematic bottom-up method for compositional CBMC unit proofs. Analysts iteratively build harnesses, ensure termination, increase coverage, and refine models of unknown variables/functions from error traces; objective completeness requires graceful termination, full reachable-line coverage, and resolution of every memory-safety violation. The study selects 73 functional units from 89 known defects across FreeRTOS, Zephyr, Contiki-ng and RIOT. Initial harnesses set loop bounds to one; coverage gaps trigger larger bounds or allocation limits, while error traces drive minimal preconditions and function models. The blind construction protocol separates defect identification from proof writing. Analyses quantify model types, loop bounds, development/execution cost, comparison with engineer-authored FreeRTOS proofs, and generalizability to larger functions.
    - number: 66/89 known defects (74%) exposed systematically; +8 (9%) after interventions; 15 (17%) not exposed
    - number: 19 new defects: 12 out-of-bound reads, 5 out-of-bound writes, one null dereference, one arithmetic underflow
    - number: Mean proof 20 LOC, 5 variable models and 2 function models; median development 72 min, execution 25 s
    - number: Average development 82 min and execution 61 s; 87.67% execute under 60 s
    - limits: CBMC bounded proofs only cover modeled scopes and chosen loop/data bounds; fixed or overly restrictive models can hide defects. The bottom-up angelic-modeling strategy may miss bugs when preconditions are too strong, and 15/89 known defects were not exposed. Authors note proof writers knew units were defective, and results study one unit-proof operationalization on four embedded OSes. Findings do not establish unbounded memory safety or generalize to other BMC tools.
    - how much was read: most of it read

A planner chooses generation modules, candidate sampling selects verified-function progress, and Verus error classes route bounded repair prompts. Code and tests are intended to remain unchanged. [Q1,Q5–Q16] Complete RingBuffer abstraction and invariant example; distinct view-refinement pass; n-sample candidate selection; mode/type/mutability/arithmetic/contract/test repair; interprocedural postcondition strengthening. Complete source evaluation, budget/model confounds, verbatim-only training-overlap claim, contradictory test-origin prose, progress regressions, Bitmap abstraction and printed repair-loop last-check boundary are recorded in ANALYSIS.md. Introduction claims LLM test generation while conclusion requires manual tests and calls automated generation future work. Resource algebra synthesis is future work; no distributed protocol or TLA+ verification. [Q1–Q3,Q17,Q20–Q23,Q28–Q29] The baseline “invokes the LLM once to generate all annotations simultaneously,” whereas VeriStruct requests n samples per module and sets n=3. The paper does not clearly reconcile candidate sampling with its unit of “LLM invocation”; the13-call cap alone does not establish equal candidate-generation effort.     - title: Reducing the Costs of Proof Synthesis on Rust Systems by Scaling Up a Seed Training Set
    - what: Presents VeruSyn, a self-synthesis, tutorial-guided and agent-trajectory pipeline producing millions of Verus-verified Rust programs and fine-tuning Qwen2.5-Coder-32B-Instruct for lower-cost proof generation. VeruSyn coordinates three data sources: direct self-synthesis of Rust/specification/proof triples, tutorial-based coverage of Verus knowledge points, and successful Claude Sonnet 4.5 trajectories with Verus error repair. Post-processing deduplicates and re-verifies programs; the resulting Part 1 has 6,896,180 programs and Part 2 has 4,557 CoT instances. Evaluation excludes the 289 system tasks used for trajectory synthesis to reduce leakage, tests VerusBench and 560 held-out VeruSAGE tasks, and separates Accuracy@1/100/6/600 with debugging. The paper reports 49% VeruSAGE Accuracy@600 at $0.61/task for VeruSyn versus 54% for Sonnet at $39.24/task, while Part 2 helps system proofs but can reduce single-sample algorithmic accuracy.

Agent trajectories use Claude Sonnet 4.5 and the evaluation directly prompts models without coding agents. Three source systems are removed from VeruSAGE evaluation, but other distribution and benchmark effects remain. Tasks exceeding Qwen context are scored zero, and Accuracy@K is a sampling metric rather than proof quality or human readability. The model's cost comparison depends on contemporaneous token prices and five-round debugging settings.

- Why Agentic Theorem Prover Works: A Statistical Provability Theory of Mathematical Reasoning Models (2026)
    - what: Develops a statistical theory for finite-budget agentic theorem proving. It models verifier-backed proof search as a deterministic finite-horizon reachability MDP and defines statistical provability as the probability that a specified prover reaches a verified proof within a verifier-call budget on a problem distribution. Under a faithful state abstraction, optimal MDP success equals budgeted syntactic provability. For offline depth-wise action-value regression followed by greedy proving, Theorem3.1 bounds the gap to the optimal prover by an occupancy-weighted sum of value-estimation errors; the multiplier is the learned prover’s average truncated proof length. Error terms separate approximation, training-region coverage geometry and Monte Carlo rollout noise, with a faster rate under an action-gap margin condition. Defines full proof states as multisets of open goals so branching remains Markov, deterministic verifier transition F, solved set G, hitting time TG, budgeted value V and average truncated length Len. Assumption3.1 requires compact concentration sets, Lipschitz optimal values and hypothesis classes, local-mass coverage of i.i.d. query pairs, conditionally independent binary rollout labels with mean Q*, and minimax regression. Theorem3.1 gives εt=approximation+covering term+Hoeffding rollout noise and regret 2Σ unsolved-mass×ε. A margin condition changes ε to ε^(β+1). Budget allocation balances Nt and mt under total labels nt=Ntmt. Appendices prove deterministic MDP, faithful abstraction, Bellman recursion, greedy regret, covering and uniform regression; truncation adds overflow penalty δW. Appendix C proposes representation selection by Bellman-certificate tightness and a complexity penalty; Appendix D interprets scaling through horizon, effective dimension and margins.
    - number: Main bound: best success probability minus learned prover's is at most 2 times (worst per-step value error) times the learned prover's average number of checker calls before success or budget end
    - number: Under an action-gap margin condition the dependence on error improves to a fast rate
    - limits: Faithful abstraction assumes e(FR(r,a))=F(e(r),a) and exact preservation of solved states. The equality between optimal MDP success and syntactic provability follows this assumption; arbitrary learned embeddings need not be faithful. The theory is trace-local. Compact concentration Ct must cover both training queries and learned-prover comparisons, and the candidate region must retain at least one globally optimal action. The paper explicitly says a new prover leaving the occupied region is no longer certified without added coverage assumptions. Binary rollout labels are idealized verifier-backed success labels with conditional mean Q*. The paper says practical-target bias can be absorbed into ε, but the stated theorem assumes exact Q* labels and its approximation term is relative to Q*. Biased targets require an explicit allowance; reader derivation using the supplied minimax proof adds2bt when label means differ uniformly by≤bt. Repeating suboptimal-policy rollouts reduces sampling noise, not this bias. No empirical calibration is supplied. The exact minimax regression optimizer is an idealization. Numerical optimization error ξt can be added, but no practical learner, neural architecture or training algorithm is evaluated. The verifier transition is deterministic, while policy decoding, retrieval and exploration may be random. The theory addresses greedy proving; beam search, backtracking, top-k and decomposition need extra retention/suboptimality assumptions. The finite-horizon result does not remove undecidability, worst-case hardness or unknown proof existence. Statistical provability depends on q0, so a biased library workload can be easy while adversarial or uniform hard instances remain difficult. The effective dimension dt, coverage ct, Lipschitz constants, action-gap tails and occupancy curve are theoretical quantities. The paper proposes estimating them from traces as future work and reports no benchmark measurement. No Verus/Rust/TLA+/distributed-system experiment is supplied. Transfer to systems verification is an inference about replacing proof-state/verifier interfaces, not a measured claim.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10538); [notes: Why Agentic Theorem Prover Works (2026)](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `why-agentic-theorem-prover-2026`
